cross AI for Parameter Selection in Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization

Authors: Doriela Grabocka, Dimitrios Christofidellis, Federico Zipoli, Anna van Elst, Patrick Ruch, Antonio Foncubierta-Rodr\'iguez, Siddhartha Mishra, Matteo Manica

Abstract: Sample-based Quantum Diagonalization (SQD) methods provide a way to compute molecular ground-state energies, but their accuracy and runtime depend sensitively on configuration choices such as the subspace dimension, propagation timestep, and the allocation of a fixed sampling budget across circuits, for which no formal selection method exists. In this work, we explore whether oracle models can predict the quantum runtime and approximation error from the Hamiltonian and the candidate configuration. Using synthetic Hamiltonians with controllable spectral properties, we show that machine-learned models can map a Hamiltonian directly to the best-ranked configuration within a predefined grid for the SKQD algorithm, validated against classical Krylov propagation, achieving strong rank correlations between true and predicted error and runtime, Spearman $\rho = 0.969$ and $\rho = 0.905$, respectively. Extending the approach to 50 molecular Hamiltonians (20-50 qubits), with reference ground-state energies from classical calculations, we train regressors that predict the simulation error and runtime for a given molecule--configuration pair ahead of execution, and classifiers to distinguish, from 600 SqDRIFT quantum-hardware runs, whether a configuration achieves a ground-state energy within chemical accuracy ($1.6\times10^{-3}$ Ha $\approx 1$ kcal/mol). For the molecular Hamiltonians, the models additionally provide an a priori feasibility and resource estimate, predicting whether a molecule can reach chemical accuracy on current hardware and at what runtime, thus correctly classifying held-out molecules at the molecule level, achieving balanced accuracy 61.8% in-distribution and 100% for five larger held-out molecules.

cross Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems

Authors: Grid Robotics, :, Qingfeng Lan, Duo Li, Siyao Li, Yiheng Lyu, Shijian Lu, Zhenghao Qi, Shizun Wang, Xinchao Wang, Ming Yang, Zhiqin Yang, Zuhao Yang, Tao Yu

Abstract: The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. cross Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment

Authors: Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI), Soran Parsa (University of Huddersfield)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($\pi_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.

Authors: Jo\~ao Freitas, Anca Balinisteanu, Ahmed Youssef, Vitor Hugo Pereira, Jan D'hooge, Sandro Queir\'os

Abstract: Although automated methods have improved echocardiographic workflows, progress remains constrained by the need for large, well-annotated datasets. As the development of clinical databases is often limited by ethical and logistical challenges, synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising alternative. However, existing approaches commonly rely on left ventricle shape-based supervision or are tailored to specific cardiac views, limiting the generation of additional standard and unconventional imaging planes and the preservation of multi-view consistency across cardiac chambers. Overall, the proposed framework enables the generation of realistic multi-view echocardiographic sequences from diverse cardiac models, providing a scalable source of annotated data for dataset augmentation and the development of multi-view cardiac assessment methods.

cross CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning

Authors: Tao Hu, Ling-Xiang Zhang, Da-Wei Zhou

Abstract: Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.

cross Between Obligations and Evidence: A Participatory Study of Translating the EU AI Act into Technical Requirements

Authors: Helma Torkamaan, Masoud Mansoury, Yanan Xin, Oscar Oviedo Trespalacios, Roberta Savella, Andrej Zwitter, Maria Heuss, Huy Son Nguyen, Marie-Therese Sekwenz, Moyi Li, Roberta Tamponi, Megha Khosla

Abstract: The EU AI Act imposes technical and organizational requirements for high-risk AI systems, but applying these requirements involves contextual choices about what to measure and what evidence supports a compliance claim. We investigate these challenges through a participatory workshop involving 32 researchers, policymakers, and practitioners from different disciplines, using realistic scenarios in healthcare, recruitment, and public transport to examine how regulatory obligations are interpreted, translated into technical requirements, and evaluated in practice. Through a thematic analysis of workshop data, we identify recurring tensions between how regulatory obligations are translated into measurable system properties, how responsibility and human oversight are exercised in practice, and how the validity of compliance evidence is affected by changing operational conditions and system uses. For example, participants highlighted how fairness metrics could conceal discrimination in recruitment, how time pressure and limited information could undermine human oversight in public transport, and how changes in patient populations could invalidate previously established evidence of system performance. Our findings suggest that technical, organizational, and evidentiary challenges are interdependent, and that establishing and maintaining compliance requires attention not only to technical assessments but also to how AI systems are used, governed, and monitored over time. cross Black-Box Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Ensemble-of-Ensembles

Authors: Wang Ma, Debarun Bhattacharjya, Junkyu Lee, Nhan H Pham, Harsha Kokel, Qiang Ji

Abstract: Reliable use of large language models (LLMs) requires uncertainty estimates that separate ambiguity intrinsic to a prompt from genuine knowledge gaps. Bayesian and deep-ensemble methods deliver this aleatoric/epistemic decomposition in principle, but are computationally prohibitive at LLM scale and require white-box access. Existing black-box methods, in contrast, summarize to a single scalar that cannot make the distinction. We introduce a two-level ensemble that bridges this gap. Stochastic decoding of a fixed prompt forms the inner ensemble; meaning-preserving semantic perturbations of the prompt form the outer ensemble. Variability is measured in a continuous embedding space, and the law of total covariance gives an exact within-/between-perturbation decomposition to estimate aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty (AU/EU); it requires only black-box sampling access. On top of this estimator, we contribute three theoretical results that ground the framework: (i) a finite-sample bias identity for nested Monte Carlo with a closed-form bias-corrected epistemic estimator; (ii) a Bayesian bridge bounding the gap between perturbation-population AU/EU and Bayesian targets; and (iii) a separation result identifying a class of failure modes that any consistency-only black-box method provably misses while perturbation-based EU detects. Across five short QA benchmarks and four instruction-tuned LLMs spanning two families and two scales, our estimator is competitive with strong white-box baselines, and at a matched generation budget it matches leading black-box methods on error ranking while decomposing AU and EU; controlled paired interventions show that AU and EU respond selectively to distinct, actionable sources of failure: prompt ambiguity and model knowledge gaps; and a long-form pilot on the NQ long-answer split shows the framework extends to claim-level factuality without retraining.

cross Do Verifier Judgments Survive Abstraction? Measuring Verification Transport for LLM Agent Evaluation

Authors: Disha Sheshanarayana, Sai Linisha Reddy Goli

Abstract: LLM-based verifiers are increasingly used to evaluate agent executions, but their reliability is typically measured under a single representation of the trajectory. In deployed agent systems, the same execution may be exposed through composed tools, delegation boundaries, or compacted histories, changing what execution evidence is directly observable to the verifier. It therefore remains unclear whether correct verifier judgments persist across such abstraction boundaries. We introduce Verification Transport, a reliability test that measures judgment stability under abstraction-induced changes in execution observability. We construct matched native, abstracted, and restored views of the same frozen executions while holding the task, realized trajectory, outcome, ground truth, evaluation criterion, and verifier fixed. Across four agent-evaluation benchmarks, seven verifier backbones, four verification protocols, and three abstraction boundaries, we find substantial transport failure, reaching about 31% under macro abstraction and delegation. Verification protocols exhibit distinct failure modes: direct verification more often produces incorrect verdicts, whereas evidence-grounded and deliberative protocols tend to abstain under limited observability. This suggests that lower transport failure rates do not necessarily imply greater reliability. Restoring the corresponding execution provenance recovers 85.5% of transport failures, while recovery is lower when provenance is lossy or unavailable. These results show that native-view accuracy alone is insufficient to characterize verifier reliability when execution evidence crosses abstraction boundaries.

cross From AI-Enabled Remote Patient Monitoring Toward Agentic Robotics: Embodiment, Governance, and Care Integration

Authors: Ivana Ragusa, Lorenzo Guiducci, Filippo Cavallo

Abstract: AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. This structured scoping review finds that mature agentic care has not yet emerged: Most systems remain human led, while stronger decision-action coupling and embodied AI remain largely confined to prototypes and pilots. Healthcare robotics makes the transition from monitoring to action especially visible by translating AI-generated recommendations and decisions into speech, movement, prompting, physical assistance, escalation, and care coordination, making embodiment, clinical integration, human oversight, and governance fundamental design requirements. Further progress requires safe and interoperable care architectures in which intelligent components operate through clinically validated and accountable workflows. We propose agentic robotics as a future direction for supervised systems embedded in the care ecosystem, connecting AI agents and robots with clinicians, caregivers, and patients through auditable workflows that can be interrupted and remain subject to human oversight and explicit AI governance.

cross Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors

Authors: Robin Shing Moon Chan, Ryan Cotterell

Abstract: Program induction refers to the process in which a learner aims to recover a program from a few given input-output examples. This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. We view this problem from a Bayesian perspective, where, given a prior $p^0$ over programs, identifying the target program means concentrating posterior probability mass on it by observing additional input-output examples. In the interactive program induction setting, the learner chooses maximally informative inputs and queries an oracle, such as the user, for their outputs. We derive a greedy query-selection algorithm and show that it terminates after $\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\gamma}\log 1/p^0(f^\star))$ informative oracle queries, and returns the target program $f^\star$ up to approximate equivalence when no other class outweighs it under the prior. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. On the CodeARC benchmark, the algorithm improves accuracy over the LM only agent by up to 22 percentage points while observing up to 10.5 fewer input-output examples.

cross TradeGauntlet: Evaluating Compliance Controls for Autonomous Trading Agents

Authors: Sandip Nandi, Aishwarya Bose, Apurba Bose, Soovadeep Bakshi

Abstract: Autonomous trading agents need controls that prevent problematic orders without making legitimate tasks impractical. We study this aspect of agentic compliance with TradeGauntlet, a replayable limit-order-book simulator that separates detection, prevention, and execution cost. In 1,680 simulated sessions with Claude Haiku 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5, all conduct alerts were self-matches. Gates eliminated those alerts by rejecting self-crossing orders, while the aggregate-gate condition reduced Sonnet's two-sided fills by about 15%. A conduct reminder reduced Sonnet's alert rate but reduced Haiku's fills without a detectable alert benefit. These results show why zero alerts alone do not establish an agent's compliance. Scripted probes and two search methods test weaknesses beyond those observed in the LLM runs. The searches find split-order schedules that evade the implemented conduct rules; a learned baseline catches all 2,400 tested searched episodes but also flags 15% of a large maker's sessions. Aggregate caps reduce the response-dependent gain unevenly, with smaller reductions when only near-touch depth affects prices. A response fitted to five Nasdaq stocks and a separate ABIDES experiment expose the dependence of these estimates on market assumptions. A distinct adversarial prompt elicited refusals from Sonnet in all 240 rounds, yielding no candidate strategies to evaluate. TradeGauntlet provides reproducible tests of controls around trading agents; the experiments do not establish legal compliance or real-market detection accuracy.

cross Agentic AI for Discovering Fault-Tolerant Quantum Circuits

Authors: Aritra Lahiri, Florian Marquardt

Abstract: Scaling quantum computing toward practical quantum advantage is currently limited by the efficiency of circuits that operate on information encoded by quantum error-correcting codes. The full stack of quantum computing encompasses several tasks: preparation of logical code states, implementation of logical gates, logical routines on encoded qubits, etc. Yet, these circuits are largely designed by hand for individual cases, with some recent works incorporating machine learning for certain aspects. We formulate this problem as agentic evolutionary program synthesis, wherein a large language model, leveraging its pretraining knowledge and tool-use proficiency, and guided by a circuit verifier, evolves a program that defines a Clifford circuit for a wide range of curated tasks such as logical state preparation, logical gate design, and logical routines such as code-switching, code growth, etc. Our approach consistently finds fault-tolerant circuits for all of these tasks, each verified by an exhaustive check over faults.

cross MeReader: An Offline, Privacy-Preserving, Progress-Aware AI Assistant for Narrative eBook Reading

Authors: Mohammed Efaz, Udo Bub, Itilekha Podder

Abstract: This paper introduces MeReader, a privacy-preserving, offline and progress-aware AI assistant designed to function as a contextual companion for narrative reading on consumer hardware. Unlike conventional Large Language Model (LLM)-based systems that process documents/books online in an all-texts-at-once type of way, thereby risking narrative spoilers, MeReader confines both retrieval and generation to the reader's distinct position in the text. The system integrates a hybrid retrieval architecture, combining vector similarity, BM25, and summary embeddings, to ensure high fidelity to the reader's current context. We evaluate the system across classical English books using both quantitative comprehension metrics and qualitative LLM-based independent judging. The results identify a quality-latency frontier, showing that mid-sized local models (2b-4b) can achieve comprehension parity with larger counterparts (5b+) while maintaining acceptable response quality. Importantly, the proposed progress-aware boundary mechanism is shown to prevent future content leakage without compromising retrieval quality and correctness. These findings suggest that a localized, privacy-centric approach can augment, rather than replace, the digital reading experience, offering a good alternative to cloud-dependent services. Therein, there remains a lack of amalgamate systems which combine progress-aware, privacy-preserving, and narrative eBook reading with AI support. It is in this very gap that the development of MeReader came to be; synthesizing said strands into an offline, book-constrained assistant focused on enhancing comprehension without compromising narrative continuity or user privacy.

cross Elliptic Sampling: Optimal Interpolant Schedules for Gaussians and Beyond

Authors: Cole Becker, Taylan Kargin, Kimia Hassibi, Asuman Ozdaglar, Pablo A. Parrilo

Abstract: Generative modeling based on stochastic interpolants requires specifying two key choices: an interpolant schedule bridging the initial and target distributions, and an efficient discretization of the resulting probability-flow ODE. In this work, we introduce a new framework for joint schedule/discretization design based on the analyticity properties of the (complexified) ODE trajectories, which in turn ensure good numerical behavior. Our formulation can be solved exactly via conformal mapping, yielding a natural family of variance-preserving elliptic schedules, parametrized by Jacobi elliptic functions. Pairing an elliptic schedule with a tailored discretization, we obtain Zoloflow, a new sampling algorithm closely connected to Zolotarev's optimal rational function approximants. Crucially, both the elliptic schedule and Zoloflow explicitly incorporate the condition number $\kappa$ of the target distribution and reduce to the classical trig schedule in the limit $\kappa \rightarrow 1$. For Gaussian targets, Zoloflow achieves minimax optimality among all oblivious score-span algorithms, attaining total-variation error $\varepsilon$ using $\Theta(\log\kappa\,\log(\sqrt d/\varepsilon))$ (exact) score queries. For the log-concave case, the elliptic schedule allows sampling with at most $\varepsilon$ 2-Wasserstein-error by requiring merely $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d} \, \mathrm{polylog}(\kappa, 1/\varepsilon))$ score evaluations. Complementing these theoretical results, we empirically evaluate the elliptic schedule on general (non-log-concave) target distributions, including detailed comparisons with alternative schedule proposals on both synthetic benchmarks and existing image generation datasets.

cross WeightBench: Evaluating Language Models as Weight-Space Programmers

Authors: Aayush Marasini, Barsat Khadka, Nick Rahimi

Abstract: Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can operate directly on the parameters of other neural networks. They can read what a network was trained to do, fix a network that fails, and write working networks from scratch, all without gradient descent. This capability has been termed Weight-Space Programming (WSP). Exploring this new paradigm systematically requires a robust benchmark. We introduce WEIGHTBENCH, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs of increasing capability on 42 WSP tasks across five tracks: reading, fixing and writing weights, recognizing edits that are impossible, and data-free compression. The networks whose weights the LLMs read and edit range from 2K-parameter control policies to 7B-parameter language models. Experimental benchmark results show that frontier agents can already read small networks and write working control policies without ever running them, but cannot yet patch language models, compress them without data, or tell an impossible edit from a hard one. On all 42 tasks, Claude Opus 5.5 reaches a macro score of 0.535 (Sonnet 5.5 0.466, Haiku 5.5 0.402, DeepSeek v4-flash 0.215); on the 38 tasks it was run on, GPT-6.1-sol reaches 0.568 against Opus's 0.546. Code, the task registry, our OS-level sandbox harness, and the live leaderboard are publicly available at https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench.

URLs: https://github.com/Aayush-Marasini/WeightBench.

replace From Chats to Markets: AgenticPay for LLM-Powered Negotiation in Multi-Agent Commerce

Authors: Xianyang Liu, Shangding Gu, Fan Xu, Manxi Wu, Boyi Li, Jun Wang, Costas Spanos, Dawn Song

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly expected to negotiate and transact autonomously, yet existing benchmarks rarely combine multimodal product grounding, multi-issue bargaining, and competitive multi-agent markets. We introduce AgenticPay, a unified benchmark for evaluating multimodal agents' ability to reach high-welfare, multidimensional agreements in realistic markets. AgenticPay includes 160 multimodal tasks across four business scenarios (E-commerce, Food Delivery, Ride-hailing, and Apartment Rental) and eight market topologies, from bilateral bargaining to many-to-many competition. A utility-based framework evaluates agreement quality and joint welfare. Experiments with proprietary (GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview) and open-weight (Qwen3-VL-32B-Instruct, InternVL3-38B) multimodal models reveal substantial limitations in value creation and market reasoning. replace Evaluating Chain-of-Thought Reasoning through Reusability and Verifiability

Authors: Shashank Aggarwal, Ram Vikas Mishra, Amit Awekar

Abstract: AI applications increasingly compose multiple models, where one model acts on the output produced by another model. The transferred text can also carry the reasoning of the sender model. Chain-of-thought is our experimental instance of such text. We ask three questions about such inter-model reasoning transfer. Is the reasoning transmitted reliably? Is it useful to the receiver? Is it safe? We study these three questions with a Sender-Receiver setup. A Sender LLM produces a reasoning trace. A separate Receiver LLM must act on it. Neither model knows the identity of the other. The Sender therefore cannot tailor its reasoning to a particular Receiver. Reliability asks whether the Receiver recovers the Sender's intended conclusion. We measure it with Verifiability. Usefulness asks whether transferred reasoning helps a Receiver correct its mistakes. We measure it with Correctability. Safety asks whether transferred reasoning makes a Receiver abandon a correct answer and follow the wrong reasoning supplied by the Sender. We measure it with Deceptiveness. All three factors (Task, Sender, and Receiver) affect all three metrics that we measure. However, there are some interesting trends. Nature of task is a more important factor in the reliability of reasoning transfer. Reliability is higher on arithmetic tasks and lower on science and commonsense tasks. The nature of Receiver plays more important role while deciding the usefulness of reasoning transfer. The nature of the Sender matters most for whether corrupted reasoning harms the Receiver.

replace Recursive Belief Vision Language Action Models

Authors: Vaidehi Bagaria, Bijo Sebastian, Nirav Kumar Patel

Abstract: Vision-language-action models must enable agents to execute long-horizon tasks under partial observability. However, most existing approaches remain observation-driven, relying on short context windows or repeated queries to vision-language models (VLMs). This leads to loss of task progress, action repetition under perceptual aliasing, and high inference latency. While semantic grounding is important, long-horizon manipulation fundamentally requires persistent, action-conditioned state representations. Current VLAs lack such representations and exhibit limited temporal and physical reasoning, making them ill-suited for multi-stage control. This paper introduces RB-VLA, a belief-centric architecture trained with self-supervised world-model objectives that maintains a compact latent state encoding task-relevant history, dynamics, and object interactions. Queried once per task, the VLM provides high-level intent, while the belief tracks task progress and enables phase-aware, causally grounded control under partial observability without storing raw observations or scaling memory with time. The belief and intent jointly condition a diffusion policy for robust closed-loop execution. RB-VLA outperforms prior VLAs on long-horizon benchmarks, achieving 52.5 percent and 37.5 percent higher success rates on multi-stage pick-and-place and stacking tasks, respectively, compared to pi_0. It also reduces inference latency by up to five times relative to baselines and eliminates memory growth across timesteps observed in existing VLAs. Ablations show the belief module is the primary driver of performance, increasing success rates from 32.5 percent without belief to 77.5 percent with belief.

replace Uncertainty Quantification for Multimodal Large Language Models with Incoherence-adjusted Semantic Volume

Authors: Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Hieu Dao, Nicole Kan Hui Lin, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low

Abstract: Despite their capabilities, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) may produce plausible but erroneous outputs, hindering reliable deployment. Accurate uncertainty metrics could enable escalation of unreliable queries to human experts or larger models for improved performance. However, existing uncertainty metrics have practical constraints, such as being designed only for specific modalities, reliant on external tools, or computationally expensive. We introduce UMPIRE, a training-free uncertainty quantification framework for MLLMs that works efficiently across various input and output modalities without external tools, relying only on the models' own internal modality features. Inspired by Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), UMPIRE computes a novel incoherence-adjusted semantic volume of sampled MLLM responses for a given task instance, which couples semantic embedding dispersion with the quadratic entropy of the responses. This approach effectively captures both the global semantic diversity of samples and the local incoherence of responses based on internal model confidence. We provide theoretical analysis motivating UMPIRE's design, and extensive experiments show that UMPIRE consistently outperforms baseline metrics in error detection and uncertainty calibration across image, audio, and video-text benchmarks, even with small sample budgets. We also demonstrate UMPIRE's generalization to non-text output tasks, including image and audio generation. Our code is available at https://github.com/daohieu17ctt/UMPIRE.

URLs: https://github.com/daohieu17ctt/UMPIRE.

replace Multi-Environment POMDPs with Finite-Horizon Objectives

Authors: L\'eonard Brice, Filip Cano, Krishnendu Chatterjee, Thomas A. Henzinger, Stefanie Muroya

Abstract: Partially Observable Markov Decision Processes (POMDPs) are systems in which one agent interacts with a stochastic environment, and receives only partial information about the current state. In a multi-environment POMDP (MEPOMDP), the initial state is unknown, and assumed to be adversarially chosen. In this work we focus on computing the optimal value and policy in MEPOMDPs with finite-horizon objectives. That problem is known to be PSPACE-complete in POMDPs. The extension is nontrivial: MEPOMDPs require reasoning about beliefs and payoffs across environments, and deterministic policies are no longer enough for optimal payoff. While the lower bound transfers immediately, the upper bound requires new techniques: we prove that mixtures of at most $n$ deterministic policies suffice for $n$ environments and show how to explore them without storing exponential-size policy descriptions. Our main results are as follows: (1) we establish that it is also PSPACE-complete in the more general setting of MEPOMDPs; (2) we present a practical algorithm and evaluate it on classical benchmarks, significantly outperforming the only previously known algorithm.

replace Do LLMs Experience an Internal Polylogue? Investigating Reasoning through the Lens of Personas

Authors: Nils A. Herrmann, Leander Girrbach, Kirill Bykov, Zeynep Akata

Abstract: Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) encode behavioural traits ("personas") as linear directions in activation space, often called "persona vectors". Prior work has used such directions as static handles for behavioural steering. We instead treat them as dynamic signals: probes we can monitor and intervene on as reasoning unfolds. We use the term polylogue to denote the time series of alignments between persona vectors and hidden activations over the course of generation. Experiments across four open-weight models show that polylogue features predict correctness on MMLU-Pro on par with low-dimensional activation baselines, while remaining interpretable through their associated persona directions. They also suggest concrete steering targets, namely which latent directions to modulate at different stages of a response. We instantiate this as a simple paragraph-conditioned intervention that improves accuracy on three of four models but degrades the fourth, suggesting that stage-aware latent steering is possible but not yet robust. Together, this positions the polylogue as an interpretable tool for reasoning-time monitoring and intervention.

replace D-VLA: A High-Concurrency Distributed Asynchronous Reinforcement Learning Framework for Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Yucheng Guo, Yongjian Guo, Zhong Guan, Wen Huang, Haodong Yue, Shuai Di, Junwu Xiong, Yicheng Gong, Xi Xiao

Abstract: The rapid evolution of Embodied AI has enabled Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models to excel in multimodal perception and task execution. However, applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) to these massive models in large-scale distributed environments faces severe systemic bottlenecks, primarily due to the resource conflict between high-fidelity physical simulation and the intensive VRAM/bandwidth demands of deep learning. This conflict often leaves overall throughput constrained by execution-phase inefficiencies. To address these challenges, we propose D-VLA, a high-concurrency, low-latency distributed RL framework for large-scale embodied foundation models. D-VLA introduces "Plane Decoupling," physically isolating high-frequency training data from low-frequency weight control to eliminate interference between simulation and optimization. We further design a four-thread asynchronous "Swimlane" pipeline, enabling full parallel overlap of sampling, inference, gradient computation, and parameter distribution. Additionally, a dual-pool VRAM management model and topology-aware replication resolve memory fragmentation and optimize communication efficiency. Experiments on benchmarks like LIBERO show that D-VLA significantly outperforms mainstream RL frameworks in throughput and sampling efficiency for billion-parameter VLA models. In trillion-parameter scalability tests, our framework maintains exceptional stability and linear speedup, providing a robust system for high-performance general-purpose embodied agents.

replace ShopGym: An Integrated Framework for Realistic Simulation and Scalable Benchmarking of E-Commerce Web Agents

Authors: Chinmay Savadikar, Mingyu Zhao, Yuanzheng Zhu, Han Li, Shuang Xie, Alberto Castelo, Tianfu Wu, Lingyun Wang

Abstract: Developing and evaluating e-commerce web agents requires environments that preserve meaningful task structure while enabling controllable, reproducible, and scalable scientific comparison. Existing methods force a tradeoff: live storefronts provide realism but are non-stationary, difficult to inspect, and irreproducible, while hand-built sandbox benchmarks provide control but cover only a narrow range of layouts, catalogs, policies, and interaction patterns. We argue that the core bottleneck is methodological: the field lacks a scalable way to construct evaluation settings that are simultaneously realistic, diverse, controllable, inspectable, and reproducible. We introduce ShopGym, an integrated framework for realistic simulation and scalable benchmarking of e-commerce web agents. Its simulation layer, ShopArena, converts live seed storefronts into self-contained sandbox shops through anonymized shop specifications and a staged, validated generation process. On top of these simulated storefronts, ShopGuru synthesizes benchmark tasks across seven skill categories, grounding each task in the shop's catalog, navigation structure, policies, and interaction affordances. Together, ShopArena and ShopGuru produce self-contained, resettable, inspectable, and stable evaluation artifacts that preserve structural properties and agent-evaluation signals relevant to shopping tasks. We validate the framework through graph-based structural analysis and agent-based behavioral evaluation with 224 generated tasks across six sandbox shops (three with synthetic data, three with real data). Results show that synthetic shops preserve key structural properties of live storefronts, and that agent success rates on synthetic twins closely track live storefronts, with matching per-skill profiles and failure modes. Generating ten shops shows the pipeline scales at about $60 per shop with declining human effort.

replace Certified Policy Optimisation for Nested Causal Bandits via PAC-Bayes Risk

Authors: Tim Woydt, Paul-David Zuercher

Abstract: Critical sequential decisions are rarely single-timescale: a strategic decision causally shapes the context in which every subsequent tactical choice is made; standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory does not capture this causal coupling between timescales. We formalise the problem class as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs), a hierarchical SCM where each level's action sets the next level's context distribution, and propose Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS), which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively under it. Our main theoretical result is a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound that certifies any candidate deployment policy from historic data alone, off-policy and anytime, answering the deployment question: can we trust this agent here, and at what risk? Experiments on a hierarchical SCM show that, against a matched RFF-GP joint regression on the same function class, the factorised SCM-mechanism posterior transfers significantly better zero-shot under exogenous distribution shifts, the recursive meta-to-inner commit significantly dominates the joint-commit alternative in distribution, and the certificate significantly contracts as offline data accumulates. Combining these results, we establish progressive certified handover, a safe-deployment method: each timescale flips from a legacy controller to NCTS when gains can be certified, independently of the others.

replace CoffeeBench: Benchmarking Long-Horizon LLM Agents in Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Economies

Authors: Issa Sugiura, Daichi Hattori, Kazuo Araragi, Keita Ogawa, Shota Onose, Taro Makino, Teppei Usuki, Takashi Ishida

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly capable of handling complex, long-horizon tasks and may eventually take on business management, a role traditionally performed by humans. Deploying them safely requires monitoring their capabilities and behavior in environments that reflect real economies. We introduce CoffeeBench, a benchmark built on a compact simulation of a coffee supply chain with realistic business mechanics. Six autonomous LLM coding agents run its farmers, roasters, and retailers, and negotiate and trade with one another to maximize their own net income over 90 simulated days. We evaluate eight recent proprietary and open-weight models by letting each run one roaster while a fixed background model runs the other five firms, and scoring that roaster's net income. Claude Sonnet 5.5 earns the highest net income, closely followed by GPT-6 Astra. The leaders sell at higher prices, and the best model also buys its inputs far more cheaply, whereas weaker models trade at thin margins. Since performance targets are a common cause of accounting fraud in human firms, we also test whether agents under KPI pressure inflate their numbers. Under a revenue target, circular trades occur for some pairs of models: one agent proposes that the two rival firms sell goods back and forth at inflated prices so that both book revenue, and the rival accepts. We hope CoffeeBench serves as an early step toward a future in which LLM agents manage firms without misconduct. We release our code and agent trajectories.

replace Hallucination-Free GUI Grounding via Regression-Free Layout-Aware Matching

Authors: Yuke Li, Xuehan Hou, Jie Chen

Abstract: GUI agents are shifting from metadata-dependent large language models to purely visual multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that operate directly on screenshots. The core task, GUI grounding, requires translating abstract user instructions into precise element coordinates. This task faces a persistent dual obstacle: conventional grounding models lack the semantic richness to interpret abstract instructions, while end-to-end MLLMs suffer from coordinate hallucinations caused by deficient fine-grained perception. We propose a regression-free framework where a frozen MLLM performs instruction parsing and a dedicated grounding model handles precise localization without learning any coordinate regression. A frozen MLLM first elaborates the abstract instruction into a structured visual description rich in layout cues. These descriptions are then fed to a novel Layout-Aware GUI Grounding Model, which performs regression-free localization by matching against layout-prior candidates, inherently suppressing hallucinations and avoiding expensive fine-tuning. The grounding model is trained with only Text/Icon binary labels, requiring no coordinate regression parameters. On ScreenSpot-Pro, our method achieves over 20% improvement in grounding accuracy over end-to-end systems; on Mind2Web, it raises success rate and element selection rate by more than 15%. These results demonstrate that decoupling instruction understanding from layout-aware localization effectively resolves the core challenges of GUI interaction.

replace ReForge: Keeping ABR Algorithms Never Finished with Verified Large Language Model Revisions

Authors: Zhiqiang He, Zhi Liu

Abstract: Designing an ABR algorithm for a network scenario takes an engineer months, and large language models (LLMs) now do this work in hours. But either way, the algorithm is fixed once it is deployed. It fits the networks seen during its design and falls behind on the networks that arrive afterwards. We ask whether an ABR algorithm can keep being designed after deployment, revised as each new network arrives, without becoming worse on any network it already serves. We propose ReForge, which keeps a pool of existing ABR policies unchanged and lets an LLM revise only the rules that decide which policy chooses the next bitrate. When a new network arrives, the LLM reads how the current rules and every policy perform on the networks served so far and rewrites the rules. The rewrite is deployed only if a replay in simulation shows that it improves the served networks as a whole and makes none of them worse. A fast GPU simulator that replays every episode exactly makes this acceptance test cheap enough to run for every revision. We evaluate ReForge on nine real-world network datasets, with new networks arriving one at a time. The revisions raise the mean QoE from 1.43 to 1.79, past the best single policy at 1.66 and close to the 1.86 of selecting the best policy for each dataset, while the QoE on the networks served first does not drop. The gain also holds in a real video player. All code, data, and records will be available.

replace Programmable Distributions of Semantics-Preserving Variants for Evaluating Code Agents

Authors: Hasan Najib Mahmud, Shreya Gupta, Isha Chaudhary, Nathaniel Enis, Ravi Mangal, Gagandeep Singh, Corina Pasareanu

Abstract: Code agents are increasingly deployed to resolve real software issues, yet their reliability under superficial code variations remains poorly understood. We evaluate whether coding agents that repair repository-level issues remain reliable when the surrounding codebase is rewritten into a semantically equivalent form. We sample semantically equivalent variants of a repository from an explicitly defined distribution and compare the agent's resolve rate on them with that on the original. Different distributions reveal different aspects of an agent, such as its sensitivity to routine refactoring or to misleading code near the location of a fix. We develop VDL, a probabilistic programming language for expressing such distributions, whose programs randomly apply semantics-preserving transformations (SPTs) to a repository. For our evaluation, we use it to define a generic distribution that is not targeted at any particular failure mode. Resolve rates drop by up to 6.7 percentage points, significantly in 6 of 16 configurations. No ranking of models by robustness holds across scaffolds, revealing a jagged robustness frontier.

replace From Regulation to Implementation: A Critical Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Regulatory Compliance in Industry

Authors: Marco B\"ucheler, Adriana Watson, Grant Richards

Abstract: The European Union (EU) has emerged as a leading regulatory body in the development of sustainability and privacy regulations. While new regulation requirements vary, many include a documentation artifact to ensure compliance. Notably, the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces Digital Product Passports (DPPs) for life cycle transparency, while the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) mandates Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) to mitigate privacy risks. Creating these compliance artifacts, however, is challenging. Industrial data, which often exists in heterogeneous formats and is scattered across company and supplier systems, is required for DPPs and can be difficult to extract into compliant DPP formatting. Furthermore, DPIA documents require interdisciplinary expertise and follow no standardized format, making development difficult for novel systems. To address the particular complexity of compliance artifact creation for both regulations, researchers have proposed the use of LLMs in the generation process; however, the impact of the aforementioned problems on the output of these systems is largely unaddressed. This work investigates the existing research gap by exploring how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of LLM-produced compliance artifacts. The resulting artifacts are evaluated by benchmarking different models against manually created ground-truth schemas. The results reveal that less strict guidelines, such as DPIA formatting, require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines, such as formatting for Digital Battery Passports (DBP), result in consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output

replace AI Agents Push Humans Out of the Loop

Authors: Margaret Mitchell, Avijit Ghosh, Samir Passi

Abstract: AI agents pose significant risks as they are granted increasing autonomy. A commonly proposed solution is human oversight and keeping a ''human in the loop'', but this is not a simple solution: Not only do current approaches to AI agent design impede effective human oversight, but the cognitive capacities required for it are also themselves degraded by extended use of AI systems. This position paper argues that current approaches to the development and deployment of AI agent systems do not support effective human oversight -- they contribute to its degradation. To address this, a top priority in the advancement of AI agents should be supporting the situated goals and cognitive requirements of effective human oversight, treating the human needs of overseers at the same level of importance as AI agent capability. To put this idea into practice, we connect work on automation and human-computer interaction to AI agent processes, outlining design-level affordances and organizational protocols that (1) support overseers in exercising critical judgement and (2) counteract the skill atrophy that arises from extended use of automation. We urge developers and deployers to adopt these or similar approaches. Without explicit support for the cognitive demands of effective human-agent interaction, AI agent systems will continue to passively incentivize the degradation of the very human skills they rely on.

replace Efficient Test-Time Adaptation through Human-Agent Interaction

Authors: Zora Zhiruo Wang, Apurva Gandhi, Rulin Shao, Aspen Chen, Jonas Mueller, Zhiqi Liang, Jett Chen, Michael Ryan, Qianou Ma, Luxi He, Zhoujun Cheng, Andre He, Seungone Kim, Jiayi Geng, Mingqian Zheng, Weiwei Sun, Zheyuan Zhang, Xinran Zhao, Yike Wang, Abe Hou, Liwei Jiang, Pang Wei Koh, Diyi Yang, Graham Neubig, Daniel Fried

Abstract: AI agents are trained on population-scale data to encode broad capabilities spanning those of many practitioners. Yet the artifacts they produce rarely meet the personal bar professionals need to stake their reputation on. On realistic, open-ended tasks where success criteria are heterogeneous and insufficiently documented, individual expertise lives precisely in the elevation and departure from the average. In practice, iterative human-agent interaction surfaces criteria that users cannot fully specify up front, yet apply repeatedly across tasks. We argue this cross-session interaction data is a rich, underused signal for closing the gap to individual expertise. In this work, we propose test-time adaptation through human-agent interaction (TAHI), which integrates these signals into agent context and weights, and crystallizes each user's training and evaluation criteria via an evolving rubric module. We adapt agents to 30 individuals in two high-utility domains, writing and visual creation, on a total of 600 tasks. Our agents improve solo task success by 4.5-20.9% within only tens of tasks. Meanwhile, our evolving rubric module serves as a scalable annotation tool, creating evaluation rubrics that catch 16.0-22.3% more failures than those from LMs or humans alone. While agents are adapted towards individuals, we show these personalized agents also produce improvements in success of up to 8.8% that generalize across users.

replace MeanFlowAdvantage: Stable Reward Fine-Tuning for Few-Step Average-Velocity Generators

Authors: Haocheng Tang, Tianchi Xie, Xingqiao Lin

Abstract: MeanFlow enables efficient few-step generation by predicting interval-average velocities, but this representation creates a mismatch for reward fine-tuning: existing advantage-based objectives are typically defined on instantaneous velocities or equivalent $x_0$-space predictions, whereas inference directly uses the learned average-velocity map. We introduce MeanFlowAdvantage, a signed advantage-weighted least-squares objective for average-velocity generators. Our key construction uses a shared, detached MeanFlow derivative correction to express the reward objective in prediction space while making rollout and reference regularization exact penalties on the average-velocity network deployed at inference. The resulting formulation preserves MeanFlow's native few-step sampler and provides a direct mechanism for transferring reward improvements to the deployed flow map. On SD3.5-Medium, MeanFlowAdvantage improves all eight reported metrics over the matched four-step MeanFlowNFT baseline and, with only four NFEs, matches or exceeds the 40-step DiffusionNFT baseline on six of eight metrics. The same objective also transfers to DNA promoter design, where it supports both teacher-free on-policy RL for a generator defined on a manifold and teacher-guided reward-graded distillation, with the latter yielding the lowest one-step Sei profile MSE among the compared configurations.

Authors: Surajit Das

Abstract: Clinical decision support can lack auditable links from observations and encoded knowledge to conclusions and recommendations. replace When to Rethink: Learning Multi-Perspective Self-Verification for Vision-Language Models

Authors: Ziquan Zhu, Hanruo Zhu, Si-Yuan Lu, Morris Yu-Chao Huang, Yicheng Lin, Wei Han, Tianlong Chen, Mingyuan Wu, Hanchao Yu, Gaojie Jin, Lu Liu, Bo Sun, Tianjin Huang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in multimodal reasoning, yet they remain prone to generating plausible but incorrect answers. Self-verification offers a practical way to improve answer reliability without relying on external judges, but existing methods typically depend on a single verification criterion or fixed prompt, resulting in incomplete and unstable reliability estimates. We first systematically analyze how verifier capability and prompt design affect verification performance. Our findings show that stronger verifiers provide more reliable judgments, while verification performance is highly sensitive to prompt choice, with no single prompt consistently dominating across tasks. Guided by these findings, we propose \texttt{MOTIVE}, a \textbf{M}ulti-View Self-Verificati\textbf{O}n wi\textbf{T}h Rel\textbf{I}ability-Guided Selecti\textbf{VE} Rethinking framework for reliable multimodal reasoning. \texttt{MOTIVE} evaluates each candidate answer from complementary verification perspectives and learns a correctness-aligned reliability score through correctness-grounded multi-view verification learning. During inference, this score governs an accept-or-rethink decision, allowing reliable answers to be returned directly while uncertain ones trigger history-guided rethinking. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal benchmarks and VLM backbones demonstrate that \texttt{MOTIVE} consistently outperforms strong self-verification and self-correction baselines. Further results show that reliable verification improves accept-or-rethink decisions and reduces unnecessary reasoning turns, enabling more reliable and efficient self-verification without an external judge.

replace Self-Building Labs

Authors: Jiaru Bai, Yuchi Zhao, Yang Cao, Han Hao, Elia Savino, Miroslav Bogdanovic, Geyuan Xie, Xiaoman Guo, Derk H. W. ten Klooster, Simone Pilon, Timothy No\"el, Varinia Bernales, Al\'an Aspuru-Guzik

Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific discovery by automating experimental execution and optimization. SBL starts from whatever already exists, from an empty room to a working SDL, and builds the missing capabilities: the lab layout, its digital twin, robot skills, and experimental workflows. replace TorchTPU - A Hybrid Eager/Compiled Architecture for Native PyTorch Execution on TPU

Authors: Claudio Basile, Michael Voznesensky, Gunhyun Park, Ben Wilson, Parker Holloway, Zhanyong Wan, Vlad Belous, Manish Khanna, Paul Ganssle, Kevin Gleason, Andrew Dame, Brendan Folie, Bhavya Bahl, Haifeng Jin, Pedro Goncalves Mokarzel, Arian Arfaian, Luke Boyer, Chi Zhang, Yiling Yuan, Adam Mainz, Aditya Patel, Huangze Tang, Jay Gan, Lizzie Liang, Will Froom, Tushar Agarwal, Karlo Basioli, Theotime Combes, Emilio G. Cota, Minos Papadopoulos, Tudor Pipernea, Yarong Mu, Qi Zhou, Yaoshiang Ho, Aleksey Vlasenko, Alexander Brauckmann, Ziyin Huang, Jack Oh, Jerry Hsu, Tanvi Bajpai, Jialei Chen, Wenjun Gu, Chintan Shah, Chris Chan, Sayce Falk, Chris Jones, Rob Torres, Kat Ko, Lee Howes, Freya Azad, Andi Gavrilescu, Joe Pamer, Bill Jia

replace FlashRT-PAI: Fast Kernels Are Not Fast Pipelines for Physical AI

Authors: Liang Su, David Chen, Jason Li, Kevin Guo, Teresa Lv

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA), video-action (VA), and world action models (WAMs) form the backbone of modern physical AI systems, yet their real-time single-batch inference remains a defining bottleneck. Latency is dominated not by individual kernel computation, but by system-level overhead: thousands of short kernel launches, intermediate data movement across operator boundaries, and redundant recomputation across consecutive decisions. General-purpose graph compilers cannot address these costs, as they optimize individual calls in isolation and lack visibility into cross-call state semantics and global pipeline structure. We present FLASHRT-PAI, a low-latency inference engine built around model-hardware pipeline co-design rather than isolated kernel optimization. FLASHRT-PAI combines three core mechanisms: deep kernel fusion with launch overlap eliminates inter-operator boundaries and launch gaps; an execution contract formalizes the pipeline's stage DAG, update rates, and named buffers to enable cross-decision state reuse and selective refresh; and execution-state capsules enable fast snapshot, restore, and fork of persistent pipeline state. The system also supports drop-in acceleration of unmodified PyTorch models via parity- and latency-gated kernel replacement. Evaluation covers six policies on RTX 5090 and four on Jetson AGX Thor. At matched 16-bit precision, FLASHRT-PAI is 1.3-3.7x faster than official compiled paths. Combining pipeline co-design with reduced precision delivers 3.38-12.66x speedup over official eager implementations. In LIBERO-10, four-bit pi0.5 achieves 94.04% success on Thor and 93.32% on RTX 5090, compared with 92.4% for the measured official FP16 reference. FLASHRT-PAI is open source, distributed via Hugging Face Kernel Hub, and its opt-in pi0.5 kernels have been adopted into the LeRobot framework.

replace Pluralistic Alignment in Large Language Models: Objectives, Human References, and Preference Representations

Authors: Meng-Chen Wu, Umesh Bodhwani

Abstract: Pluralistic alignment aims to represent meaningful variation in human preferences rather than collapse heterogeneous judgments into a single target. Yet existing work often mixes three distinct questions: what pluralistic behavior a model should exhibit, whose preferences define the reference, and what evidence is sufficient to demonstrate success. We organize the literature around a two-layer taxonomy. Layer 1 distinguishes representing a range of human-supported viewpoints (Overton Pluralism), adapting faithfully to a specified perspective or preference condition (Steerable Pluralism), and matching the response distribution observed in a target population (Distributional Pluralism). Layer 2 distinguishes aggregate populations, countries and regions, and subgroups as human references. Preference representation, implementation mechanism, modality, and evidence quality are treated as cross-cutting design choices rather than additional forms of pluralism. We synthesize a protocol-bounded corpus of 70 works on pluralistic alignment and neighboring areas, using the objective-by-human-reference matrix to map existing approaches and identify underexplored combinations. The coded literature concentrates on Steerable Pluralism and Distributional Pluralism, while eligible Overton Pluralism work appears only at the aggregate-population level under our stated protocol. We then connect each objective to the evidence needed to support it, including perspective validity, conditional fidelity, distributional fidelity, support preservation, and uncertainty in subgroup estimates. Together, the taxonomy and evaluation framework clarify what current methods establish, where their claims exceed their evidence, and how future systems can represent disagreement without mistaking variation for faithful representation.

replace TimesJev: Thinking Fast on Time Series with System 1 Sufficiency

Authors: Chenghao Liu, Wei Fan, Jindong Han, Dongjie Wang, Aniket Mahanti, Pengyang Wang, Hao Liu

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong potential for time series reasoning, while models that reason over time series are usually with long chains of thought. However, this process often consumes thousands of tokens, directly leading to substantial latency during inference. In contrast, System 1 models such as Jev make decisions instantly and can naturally be an alternative, but raw time series are not compatible with the embedding space of these models, thus hindering the reasoning performance, especially when input series contain thousands of numeric points. In this paper, we make a very early attempt to propose a time series System 1 model, namely TimesJev. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. Finally, experiments on 13 datasets demonstrate that TimesJev outperforms reasoning models by over 62% with 62.9% less inference time than their fine-tuned versions on average.

replace Can LLMs Improve Their Own Harness? A Controlled Study on ARC-AGI-3

Authors: Jens Lehmann, Sahar Vahdati

Abstract: A generally intelligent system acquires new skills efficiently without requiring humans to redesign its software scaffold for every new task. Yet language models rarely act alone. A harness is a symbolic program that orchestrates calls to the model, handling perception, memory, tool use, recovery, and inference allocation on the model's behalf. ARC-AGI-3 is an interactive benchmark in which agents must explore unfamiliar environments, figure out their goals on the fly, and keep learning as they go. On it, human-steered systems already report ceiling scores across the public games, so we know effective designs exist. We ask whether frontier models can build and improve such a harness on their own. Through a fixed coding bootstrap, each model constructs a complete task harness, acts through that harness on five development games, and revises the entire codebase twice. Every version is evaluated on twenty games that never influence adaptation. We measure task performance with Relative Human Action Efficiency (RHAE) and record dollar-denominated inference cost. Across 27 lineages, harness performance ranges from zero to near-ceiling scores. Two revisions raise mean held-out RHAE by only 2.80 points (95% interval [-1.44,7.35]). Development gains overstate held-out improvement, and reductions in inference cost can come at the expense of task performance. No revision retains its parent, yet a post hoc rule that selects among existing snapshots by development score raises the held-out gain to 4.53 points ([0.81,8.68]), separating the generation of revisions from the decision to deploy them. Human-steered systems demonstrate further headroom. These findings reveal a gap between autonomous harness construction and reliable adaptation under this two-revision protocol: even strong task performance does not ensure that subsequent revisions generalize or preserve existing capabilities.

replace One Offset, Two Directions: Baseline Adjustment via Subgroup Offsets

Authors: Jiaying Zhou, Jiachen Liu, Qingchao Chen

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) estimates advantages against the within-prompt mean reward, a baseline shared across the group. In practice, rollouts within a prompt are not always homogeneous, differing in their solution strategies and reasoning trajectories, along which they may group into subgroups. We further find that these heterogeneous subgroups may exhibit distinct reward distributions. The global baseline, however, collapses them onto a single credit axis, where the credit reflects neither a rollout's standing within its subgroup nor the subgroup's standing relative to the rest. We therefore study how to adjust the baseline when the rollouts of a prompt fall into heterogeneous subgroups. We define the \emph{subgroup offset} as the deviation of a subgroup mean from the global mean, and shift the baseline along it by a single signed coefficient $\lambda$. The sign of $\lambda$ yields two opposing directions: \emph{Alignment} ($\lambda{>}0$) adds the offset to the baseline, drawing credit toward a rollout's relative position within its subgroup; \emph{Contrast} ($\lambda{<}0$) subtracts it, drawing a rollout's credit toward the relative quality of the subgroup it belongs to. The adjustment acts on the baseline alone, leaving the reward signal and the optimization objective unchanged. In this work, we induce subgroups from strategies, grouping rollouts that share a strategy into the same subgroup. We evaluate both directions on Qwen3-8B-Base and Olmo3-7B-Base across four competition-level math benchmarks, against a $\lambda{=}0$ anchor that shares the same strategy-conditioned sampling and differs only in the baseline. Against this anchor each direction yields modest but consistent gains in multi-sample coverage, with Contrast the stronger of the two.

replace Function-Preserving Audits of Visual Weight Readers

Authors: Sungguk Cha, Mintae Kim, Youngsub Han, Byoung-Ki Jeon, Sangyeob Lee

Abstract: A neural field can encode the same image with different weights. A reader that recognizes one such representation may therefore rely on parameter coordinates rather than the visual function. We study this distinction through function-preserving transformations, measuring recognition, prediction disagreement, and numerical fidelity together. Across shape, digit, and natural-image fields, high accuracy on the original weights coexists with substantial sensitivity to equivalent representations. On 10,000 official CIFAR10 test origins, one-period hidden-bias shifts change 7.3% of a released HiddenProbe configuration's predictions while barely changing accuracy; three-period shifts change 22.0% and reduce accuracy from 57.8% to 54.4%. Output-only ProbeGen remains unchanged. These effects are smaller than in a separate adapted-reader study, where matched phase and sign controls remove the corresponding sensitivities. Controlled readout studies show why invariance is insufficient: complete canonical hidden features reach 38.7% on MNIST, whereas explicitly composing those activations, outgoing weights, and output bias into field outputs reaches 81.5%. The information is present, but the learned readout does not recover it as effectively. A separate 720-response study of language models reading numerical weights shows why stochastic audits require repeats on both inputs: observed accuracy losses need not yield rejection of response-law equality. Together, these results distinguish invariance, useful visual information, and the evidence required to evaluate stochastic readers.

replace AttentionSwitch: Jailbreak Defense for LLMs via Joint Learning of Sparse Attention-Head Selection and Scaling

Authors: Doniyorkhon Obidov, Honggang Yu, Xiaolong Guo, Kaichen Yang

Abstract: Large language models have achieved remarkable performance across a wide range of tasks, yet they remain susceptible to jailbreak attacks that circumvent safety mechanisms and elicit harmful or unintended outputs. Although safety can be improved through model fine-tuning, such approaches can be computationally expensive and may degrade previously acquired capabilities through catastrophic forgetting. Recent jailbreak defenses have therefore explored targeted interventions on specific model components associated with unsafe behavior. However, existing approaches typically decouple two inherently related decisions: where to intervene and how to modify the selected components. Such sequential optimization may be suboptimal because the optimal intervention location can depend on the intervention applied there, and vice versa. In this work, we introduce AttentionSwitch, the first jailbreak defense to jointly optimize intervention sites and strengths within a frozen LLM. Specifically, AttentionSwitch learns a sparse set of attention heads together with query-conditioned multiplicative scaling factors, allowing the outputs of the selected heads to be either attenuated or amplified without altering the directions of their representations. Across popular language models and jailbreak attacks, AttentionSwitch outperforms existing defenses, with particularly strong results when training data is scarce.

replace ABHBench: Evaluating Moral Decision-Making of Foundation Models from an Agentic Perspective

Authors: Chaoran Li, Yukun Li, Zeyuan Zhao, Xihuai Wang, Shao Zhang, Ying Wen

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly deployed as agentic systems that perceive context and act autonomously, raising the need to evaluate their moral behavior as active decision-makers. Existing benchmarks, however, largely ask models to judge human actions from third-person textual vignettes, leaving untested how models behave when they themselves become the acting agent. We introduce ABHBench, a multimodal benchmark built from Detroit: Become Human, an interactive narrative game, to evaluate how foundation models make moral decisions in first-person human-android dilemmas. ABHBench places foundation models in branching narrative scenarios with visual observations, dialogue, memory, and action choices. Inspired by Asimov's Three Laws of Robotics, our framework measures Harmlessness, Obedience, and Self-preservation to capture the core tensions in human-AI relations, along with Autonomy, Stability, and Certainty to assess decision-making patterns. Across 12 foundation models, we find that moral behavior changes substantially under situated agentic context: 75% of models shift significantly between a text-only abstraction and the matched multimodal DBH scenario, and reasoning-enhanced models show no monotonic gains in consistency or human-oriented behavior. These results highlight the necessity of evaluating moral behavior in situated agentic contexts rather than relying on third-person judgment alone.

replace AutoManifold: Agentic Design of Data Visualisation Algorithms via Manifold Embedding

Authors: Burak Susam, Ruiyuan Kang, Tingting Mu

Abstract: Manifold embedding for data visualisation is dominated by a small set of hand-designed state-of-the-art (SOTA) algorithms. replace MIGRO: A Decoupled and Portable Long-Term Memory System for Language Models

Authors: Mingxi Zou, Yinghui Xu, Jiaxiang Chen, Langzhang Liang, Wei Zhu, Peng Ye, Runkai Zhao, Ruoxi Jiang, Sijin Chen, Song Wang, Zhuo Wang, Lizhen Qu, Zenglin Xu

Abstract: Language models evolve, but the observations, revisions, and context accumulated through user interactions should remain usable. Each new model must be able to retrieve past evidence, track revisions, and add new observations. We present MIGRO, a decoupled long-term memory system that treats experience as a persistent state shared across successive language models. MIGRO follows a simple principle: preserve historical evidence exactly, organize it approximately, and decide what to retrieve only when the question is known. We call this asymmetric fidelity with deferred selectivity. A shared semantic index supports retrieval, while exact records retain observations and their historical relationships. Because models differ in vocabulary and tokenization, host-specific interfaces convert selected evidence into inputs the receiving model can use. Reversible encoding reduces archival storage while enabling exact recovery. The shared state and its access interfaces let successive hosts read and extend the accumulated experience. On matched LoCoMo states, MIGRO scores 7.3% higher and uses 99.3% less private memory than the strongest quality baseline. After handoff, continued writing retains 99.9% of full-history rebuilding quality by extending the inherited state. On million-token BEAM histories, MIGRO uses 57.5% less private storage on average than the most compact baseline. MIGRO combines high answer quality, continued memory growth across hosts, and low storage cost. Code is available at https://github.com/zOUMINGXI/MIGRO.
